What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally made me come in is the tension in my body. My neck and shoulders are tight almost all the time now, and even my jaw feels clenched a lot of the day. It’s like I’m bracing myself for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening—just sitting at my desk at school, or trying to relax and watch TV in the evening.\n\nI kept thinking it would ease off if I stretched or just got through the week, but it’s become so constant that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any pain in your body?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly in my neck and shoulders, and my jaw too. It’s not like a sharp pain exactly, more this constant tight, aching tension, like I’m holding myself stiff without realizing it. By the time I notice it, my shoulders are practically up around my ears and my jaw is clenched.\n\nIt happens even when there’s no real reason for it—just sitting at my desk at school marking work, or at home trying to watch TV at night. It’s like my body is bracing for bad news all the time, and I can’t quite get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Is this tension and pain affecting your ability to do your schoolwork or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is, more than I’d like to admit, honestly. At school I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which is not really like me. I used to be able to stay on top of it, or at least catch up in the evenings, but lately I’ll sit down with a stack of papers or try to plan the next lesson and I just… I don’t get through it the way I should.\n\nAnd outside of school too. I’ve been involved with community theater for years, mostly helping manage things behind the scenes, and I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I kept telling myself I’d just push through, but it got to where I was letting things slide, and that made me feel even worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. By the time the last bell rings, I feel absolutely wrung out, and not just in the normal “long day at work” kind of way. It’s more like I’ve been pushing myself uphill all day without realizing it.\n\nEven when I get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or I have a quiet Sunday where I don’t do much, I don’t really feel restored. I’ll sit down to grade what should be a short stack of quizzes, something I’ve done thousands of times, and it feels like pushing through mud. I keep thinking, “This shouldn’t be this hard,” but my energy just feels used up before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I have low days, of course—who doesn’t—but it doesn’t feel like a deep sadness is the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get worn down and discouraged when I can’t keep up with things the way I used to, and then I’ll feel bad about myself for that. But if you mean feeling sad most of the time, or like I’m in a dark mood all day, I don’t think that’s really it. I haven’t noticed that in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much so. I feel wound up a lot of the day, like my body is bracing for something even when I’m just doing ordinary things. It’s this constant sense that something else is about to go wrong, or that I’ve forgotten something important.\n\nAt school, I’ll catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods instead of just sitting at my desk for a minute like I used to. I’ll walk to the board, then back to my desk, then over to the window, and I’m not even really doing anything productive. And I check my phone more than I should, especially for messages from the contractor. I know checking it every few minutes doesn’t help, but I still do it, like I can’t quite settle until I know whether there’s another problem waiting for me.\n\nIt’s not really a dramatic panic feeling, exactly. It’s more like I’m keyed up and waiting for the next thing to land on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when things are technically going all right, my mind doesn’t seem to accept that and just settle down.\n\nIt sort of hops from one thing to the next. I’ll think, okay, the contractors seem to have that part of the house under control… but then I start wondering, did they miss something, did I fail to ask the right question, is there some hidden problem that’s going to cost more later? And then before I’ve even finished that thought, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are getting sloppy, whether I’m falling behind in what I’m supposed to cover, whether the students can tell I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then it branches out again. I worry about the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been giving them enough attention, or whether I’m letting them down. And then I’ll go home and I’m not even really present because I’m thinking about whether I’m disappointing my wife, my family, by not keeping everything under control the way I usually try to.\n\nSo yes… even when nothing is actively falling apart, I’m still scanning for what might be. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your anxiety or tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head. I tend to tell myself I’m prioritizing, or being realistic, or that I’ll get back to things once things calm down. But if I’m honest, I’ve stopped taking on some things because I feel too tense and overwhelmed to manage them.\n\nAt school, for example, I’m falling behind on lesson prep and grading in a way that really bothers me. I used to be the kind of teacher who had materials ready ahead of time and could keep the grading moving along steadily. Lately I’ll put it off because just looking at the stack or opening the planning file makes me feel like I’m already behind before I’ve even started. So I avoid it for a while, which of course makes it worse.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve pulled back from parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I didn’t quit everything, but I gave up some of the responsibilities I normally would have handled because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I felt bad doing that, because people rely on me, and I’m used to being someone who says yes and figures it out. But lately the thought of adding one more thing to coordinate just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying focused on a few specific areas, like work or home, or does it seem to spread across many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It spreads. I mean, it may start with one thing—usually the house lately, because the contractor situation is right there in front of me—but it doesn’t stay there.\n\nI’ll be thinking, “What if they missed something? What if I didn’t catch some detail I should have asked about?” And then somehow that turns into school, like, “Are my lesson plans getting thin? Am I slipping and just not noticing it?” Then I’ll start thinking about the robotics kids and whether they’re falling behind because I haven’t been as available or as organized with them.\n\nAnd then it follows me home, too. I’ll be sitting there with my wife and family, but in my head I’m still going over whether I’m letting everyone down because I can’t keep all the pieces under control the way I usually try to. So it’s not just one neat category. It kind of jumps tracks from the house to work to students to family, and by the time I notice it, I’ve been worrying about half my life at once.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel completely free from worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I keep trying to think of one area where I can say, “No, that’s fine, I’m not worried about that,” but my mind doesn’t seem to leave anything alone for very long.\n\nEven if I’m not actively dealing with one problem, it sort of moves on to the next. Like, I’ll start with the house and whether the contractors missed something or whether I failed to catch some detail. Then before I know it, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not keeping up the way I should. And then it goes to the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been as on top of that as I normally am.\n\nAnd then at home, instead of feeling like I can just relax, I start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So… no, I don’t really have a place where I feel completely free of it. It feels like it follows me around and just changes subjects.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious in social situations, like when you're with your colleagues or in a group of people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say social situations are the main thing for me. Then before I know it, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not keeping up the way I should. And then it goes to the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been as on top of that as I normally am.\n\nAnd then at home, instead of feeling like I can just relax, I start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So… no, I don’t really have a place where I feel completely free of it. It feels like it follows me around and just changes subjects.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious in social situations, like when you're with your colleagues or in a group of people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say social situations are the main thing for me. I mean, I can be tense around colleagues sometimes, especially if there’s a meeting or a group conversation where I feel like I should have an answer ready, but I don’t know that I avoid people because of that specifically.\n\nWith colleagues, I’m usually able to function. I talk, I answer questions, I do what I need to do. It’s more that I might be in my head while I’m doing it, wondering if I’m coming across as scattered or if I’ve forgotten something I was supposed to follow up on. But being with people itself isn’t usually the thing that sets it off, at least not in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, not especially in social situations just for the sake of being social. I haven’t really noticed it as a separate pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience almost every day, regardless of what's happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s pretty much almost every day. It doesn’t always feel like the same worry, if that makes sense, but the worrying itself is there most days.\n\nSome days it starts right away with the house—like I’ll be thinking about whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail before it became a problem. Then even if I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve thought about that enough,” my mind just shifts to school. I’ll start wondering if my lesson plans are getting thin, or if I’m slipping and not realizing it. Then it moves to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re falling behind because I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then even when I’m home, when nothing is actually happening in that moment, I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So yes, it’s not really dependent on one specific crisis happening. It’s more like my mind keeps finding something to latch onto, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you feel you can do without worrying about the outcome or potential problems?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and… honestly, not much comes to mind. Even things that should be simple or familiar, I seem to turn them into something I need to check or anticipate or prevent from going wrong.\n\nLike with the house, I can’t just think, “Okay, the work is being handled.” My mind goes straight to whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail earlier. And then if I try to put that aside, I don’t really feel free of the worry—it just switches tracks. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping or whether I’m not preparing as well as I should.\n\nThen there’s the robotics kids, and I’ll worry they’re behind because maybe I haven’t been as organized or as present as I normally am. And at home, even when I should be able to just sit with my wife and family, I start thinking I’m disappointing them because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can do things, I get through them, but doing them without worrying about the outcome or what could go wrong? That’s the part that feels hard to find.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing that's happening, or is it more of a general feeling that something could go wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think… more like a general feeling that something could go wrong, even if there isn’t one clear thing happening right then.\n\nI mean, there are specific topics my mind grabs onto, but it doesn’t stay neatly with one problem. It’ll start with the house, like wondering if the contractors missed something or if I should have caught some issue earlier. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is, especially with my family, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it’s not like there’s been some huge blowup or anything. It’s more that I’m not as present as I used to be.

At school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that spills over at home because I’m bringing more of it back with me mentally. Even if I’m sitting there with my wife or trying to be with the family, part of me is still thinking, “I need to catch up, I’m behind, I’m letting things slide.” So I’m there, but I’m not really there, if that makes sense.

And outside of work, I used to help manage parts of the community theater stuff—organizing things, keeping track of details, being one of the people others could rely on. I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated doing that, because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and those are people I care about.

So yes, it’s affecting relationships, not necessarily because people are angry with me, but because I’m pulling back from things and not carrying what I normally would.

It’s not like a sharp pain exactly, more this constant tight, aching tension, like I’m holding myself stiff without realizing it. It’s like my body is bracing for bad news all the time, and I can’t quite get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Is this tension and pain affecting your ability to do your schoolwork or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is, more than I’d like to admit, honestly. At school I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which is not really like me. I used to be able to stay on top of it, or at least catch up in the evenings, but lately I’ll sit down with a stack of papers or try to plan the next lesson and I just… I don’t get through it the way I should.\n\nAnd outside of school too. By the time the last bell rings, I feel absolutely wrung out, and not just in the normal “long day at work” kind of way. It’s more like I’ve been pushing myself uphill all day without realizing it.\n\nEven when I get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or I have a quiet Sunday where I don’t do much, I don’t really feel restored. I’ll sit down to grade what should be a short stack of quizzes, something I’ve done thousands of times, and it feels like pushing through mud. I keep thinking, “This shouldn’t be this hard,” but my energy just feels used up before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I have low days, of course—who doesn’t—but it doesn’t feel like a deep sadness is the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get worn down and discouraged when I can’t keep up with things the way I used to, and then I’ll feel bad about myself for that. But if you mean feeling sad most of the time, or like I’m in a dark mood all day, I don’t think that’s really it. I haven’t noticed that in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much so. I feel wound up a lot of the day, like my body is bracing for something even when I’m just doing ordinary things. It’s this constant sense that something else is about to go wrong, or that I’ve forgotten something important.\n\nAt school, I’ll catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods instead of just sitting at my desk for a minute like I used to. I’ll walk to the board, then back to my desk, then over to the window, and I’m not even really doing anything productive. And I check my phone more than I should, especially for messages from the contractor. I know checking it every few minutes doesn’t help, but I still do it, like I can’t quite settle until I know whether there’s another problem waiting for me.\n\nIt’s not really a dramatic panic feeling, exactly. It’s more like I’m keyed up and waiting for the next thing to land on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when things are technically going all right, my mind doesn’t seem to accept that and just settle down.\n\nIt sort of hops from one thing to the next. I’ll think, okay, the contractors seem to have that part of the house under control… but then I start wondering, did they miss something, did I fail to ask the right question, is there some hidden problem that’s going to cost more later? And then before I’ve even finished that thought, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are getting sloppy, whether I’m falling behind in what I’m supposed to cover, whether the students can tell I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then it branches out again. I worry about the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been giving them enough attention, or whether I’m letting them down. And then I’ll go home and I’m not even really present because I’m thinking about whether I’m disappointing my wife, my family, by not keeping everything under control the way I usually try to.\n\nSo yes… even when nothing is actively falling apart, I’m still scanning for what might be. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your anxiety or tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head. I tend to tell myself I’m prioritizing, or being realistic, or that I’ll get back to things once things calm down. But if I’m honest, I’ve stopped taking on some things because I feel too tense and overwhelmed to manage them.\n\nAt school, for example, I’m falling behind on lesson prep and grading in a way that really bothers me. I used to be the kind of teacher who had materials ready ahead of time and could keep the grading moving along steadily. Lately I’ll put it off because just looking at the stack or opening the planning file makes me feel like I’m already behind before I’ve even started. So I avoid it for a while, which of course makes it worse.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve pulled back from parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I didn’t quit everything, but I gave up some of the responsibilities I normally would have handled because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I felt bad doing that, because people rely on me, and I’m used to being someone who says yes and figures it out. But lately the thought of adding one more thing to coordinate just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying focused on a few specific areas, like work or home, or does it seem to spread across many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It spreads. I mean, it may start with one thing—usually the house lately, because the contractor situation is right there in front of me—but it doesn’t stay there.\n\nI’ll be thinking, “What if they missed something? What if I didn’t catch some detail I should have asked about?” And then somehow that turns into school, like, “Are my lesson plans getting thin? Am I slipping and just not noticing it?” Then I’ll start thinking about the robotics kids and whether they’re falling behind because I haven’t been as available or as organized with them.\n\nAnd then it follows me home, too. I’ll be sitting there with my wife and family, but in my head I’m still going over whether I’m letting everyone down because I can’t keep all the pieces under control the way I usually try to. So it’s not just one neat category. It kind of jumps tracks from the house to work to students to family, and by the time I notice it, I’ve been worrying about half my life at once.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel completely free from worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I keep trying to think of one area where I can say, “No, that’s fine, I’m not worried about that,” but my mind doesn’t seem to leave anything alone for very long.\n\nEven if I’m not actively dealing with one problem, it sort of moves on to the next. Like, I’ll start with the house and whether the contractors missed something or whether I failed to catch some detail. Then before I know it, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not keeping up the way I should. And then it goes to the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been as on top of that as I normally am.\n\nAnd then at home, instead of feeling like I can just relax, I start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So… no, I don’t really have a place where I feel completely free of it. It feels like it follows me around and just changes subjects.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious in social situations, like when you're with your colleagues or in a group of people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say social situations are the main thing for me. I mean, I can be tense around colleagues sometimes, especially if there’s a meeting or a group conversation where I feel like I should have an answer ready, but I don’t know that I avoid people because of that specifically.\n\nWith colleagues, I’m usually able to function. I talk, I answer questions, I do what I need to do. It’s more that I might be in my head while I’m doing it, wondering if I’m coming across as scattered or if I’ve forgotten something I was supposed to follow up on. But being with people itself isn’t usually the thing that sets it off, at least not in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, not especially in social situations just for the sake of being social. I haven’t really noticed it as a separate pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience almost every day, regardless of what's happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s pretty much almost every day. It doesn’t always feel like the same worry, if that makes sense, but the worrying itself is there most days.\n\nSome days it starts right away with the house—like I’ll be thinking about whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail before it became a problem. Then even if I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve thought about that enough,” my mind just shifts to school. I’ll start wondering if my lesson plans are getting thin, or if I’m slipping and not realizing it. Then it moves to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re falling behind because I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then even when I’m home, when nothing is actually happening in that moment, I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So yes, it’s not really dependent on one specific crisis happening. It’s more like my mind keeps finding something to latch onto, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you feel you can do without worrying about the outcome or potential problems?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and… honestly, not much comes to mind. Even things that should be simple or familiar, I seem to turn them into something I need to check or anticipate or prevent from going wrong.\n\nLike with the house, I can’t just think, “Okay, the work is being handled.” My mind goes straight to whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail earlier. And then if I try to put that aside, I don’t really feel free of the worry—it just switches tracks. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping or whether I’m not preparing as well as I should.\n\nThen there’s the robotics kids, and I’ll worry they’re behind because maybe I haven’t been as organized or as present as I normally am. And at home, even when I should be able to just sit with my wife and family, I start thinking I’m disappointing them because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can do things, I get through them, but doing them without worrying about the outcome or what could go wrong? That’s the part that feels hard to find.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing that's happening, or is it more of a general feeling that something could go wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think… more like a general feeling that something could go wrong, even if there isn’t one clear thing happening right then.\n\nI mean, there are specific topics my mind grabs onto, but it doesn’t stay neatly with one problem. It’ll start with the house, like wondering if the contractors missed something or if I should have caught some issue earlier. Then I’ll try to reason with myself and say, “Okay, there’s nothing you can do about that right now,” but then it just shifts to school. I’ll start thinking my lesson plans might be slipping, or that I’m not preparing the way I should.\n\nAnd then it moves again to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re behind because I haven’t been organized enough or attentive enough. Then at home I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo it’s not usually just one specific event. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for where something might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your anxiety is affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, especially with my family, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it’s not like there’s been some huge blowup or anything. It’s more that I’m not as present as I used to be.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that spills over at home because I’m bringing more of it back with me mentally. Even if I’m sitting there with my wife or trying to be with the family, part of me is still thinking, “I need to catch up, I’m behind, I’m letting things slide.” So I’m there, but I’m not really there, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd outside of work, I used to help manage parts of the community theater stuff—organizing things, keeping track of details, being one of the people others could rely on. I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. Even things that should be simple or familiar, I seem to turn them into something I need to check or anticipate or prevent from going wrong.\n\nLike with the house, I can’t just think, “Okay, the work is being handled.” My mind goes straight to whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail earlier. And then if I try to put that aside, I don’t really feel free of the worry—it just switches tracks. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping or whether I’m not preparing as well as I should.\n\nThen there’s the robotics kids, and I’ll worry they’re behind because maybe I haven’t been as organized or as present as I normally am. And at home, even when I should be able to just sit with my wife and family, I start thinking I’m disappointing them because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can do things, I get through them, but doing them without worrying about the outcome or what could go wrong? That’s the part that feels hard to find.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing that's happening, or is it more of a general feeling that something could go wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think… more like a general feeling that something could go wrong, even if there isn’t one clear thing happening right then.\n\nI mean, there are specific topics my mind grabs onto, but it doesn’t stay neatly with one problem. It’ll start with the house, like wondering if the contractors missed something or if I should have caught some issue earlier. Then I’ll try to reason with myself and say, “Okay, there’s nothing you can do about that right now,” but then it just shifts to school. I’ll start thinking my lesson plans might be slipping, or that I’m not preparing the way I should.\n\nAnd then it moves again to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re behind because I haven’t been organized enough or attentive enough. Then at home I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo it’s not usually just one specific event. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for where something might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your anxiety is affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, especially with my family, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it’s not like there’s been some huge blowup or anything. It’s more that I’m not as present as I used to be.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that spills over at home because I’m bringing more of it back with me mentally. Even if I’m sitting there with my wife or trying to be with the family, part of me is still thinking, “I need to catch up, I’m behind, I’m letting things slide.” So I’m there, but I’m not really there, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd outside of work, I used to help manage parts of the community theater stuff—organizing things, keeping track of details, being one of the people others could rely on. I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated doing that, because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and those are people I care about.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting relationships, not necessarily because people are angry with me, but because I’m pulling back from things and not carrying what I normally would.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your anxiety is interfering with your ability to enjoy time with friends or engage in hobbies you used to like?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I've started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and I gave up parts of the community-theater work I usually manage because I couldn't keep up with all of it anymore.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it is. I mean, I still try to do things, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped functioning, but it’s definitely gotten in the way.

A big part of it is that I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that sort of hangs over everything. Even if I’m technically doing something enjoyable, part of my mind is still back at school thinking, “You should be catching up, you should be more prepared, you’re letting things pile up.” So it’s hard to actually enjoy the time, because I feel like I haven’t earned it, I guess.

And with hobbies or community things… the community-theater work is probably the clearest example. I used to manage parts of that, the organizing and keeping track of details, and I liked being involved. It made me feel useful and connected. But I gave up parts of it because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I was worried I’d drop something or not follow through properly, and eventually it felt like I had to step back.

So yes, it’s interfering. Not because I don’t care about those things anymore — I do — but because I can’t seem to relax into them the way I used to.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally made me come in is the tension in my body. My neck and shoulders are tight almost all the time now, and even my jaw feels clenched a lot of the day. It’s like I’m bracing myself for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening—just sitting at my desk at school, or trying to relax and watch TV in the evening.\n\nI kept thinking it would ease off if I stretched or just got through the week, but it’s become so constant that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any pain in your body?\n\nPatient: Yes—mostly in my neck and shoulders, and my jaw too. It’s not like a sharp pain exactly, more this constant tight, aching tension, like I’m holding myself stiff without realizing it. By the time I notice it, my shoulders are practically up around my ears and my jaw is clenched.\n\nIt happens even when there’s no real reason for it—just sitting at my desk at school marking work, or at home trying to watch TV at night. It’s like my body is bracing for bad news all the time, and I can’t quite get it to let go.\n\nDoctor: Is this tension and pain affecting your ability to do your schoolwork or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is, more than I’d like to admit, honestly. At school I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which is not really like me. I used to be able to stay on top of it, or at least catch up in the evenings, but lately I’ll sit down with a stack of papers or try to plan the next lesson and I just… I don’t get through it the way I should.\n\nAnd outside of school too. I’ve been involved with community theater for years, mostly helping manage things behind the scenes, and I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I kept telling myself I’d just push through, but it got to where I was letting things slide, and that made me feel even worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. By the time the last bell rings, I feel absolutely wrung out, and not just in the normal “long day at work” kind of way. It’s more like I’ve been pushing myself uphill all day without realizing it.\n\nEven when I get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or I have a quiet Sunday where I don’t do much, I don’t really feel restored. I’ll sit down to grade what should be a short stack of quizzes, something I’ve done thousands of times, and it feels like pushing through mud. I keep thinking, “This shouldn’t be this hard,” but my energy just feels used up before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I have low days, of course—who doesn’t—but it doesn’t feel like a deep sadness is the main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get worn down and discouraged when I can’t keep up with things the way I used to, and then I’ll feel bad about myself for that. But if you mean feeling sad most of the time, or like I’m in a dark mood all day, I don’t think that’s really it. I haven’t noticed that in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much so. I feel wound up a lot of the day, like my body is bracing for something even when I’m just doing ordinary things. It’s this constant sense that something else is about to go wrong, or that I’ve forgotten something important.\n\nAt school, I’ll catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods instead of just sitting at my desk for a minute like I used to. I’ll walk to the board, then back to my desk, then over to the window, and I’m not even really doing anything productive. And I check my phone more than I should, especially for messages from the contractor. I know checking it every few minutes doesn’t help, but I still do it, like I can’t quite settle until I know whether there’s another problem waiting for me.\n\nIt’s not really a dramatic panic feeling, exactly. It’s more like I’m keyed up and waiting for the next thing to land on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when things are technically going all right, my mind doesn’t seem to accept that and just settle down.\n\nIt sort of hops from one thing to the next. I’ll think, okay, the contractors seem to have that part of the house under control… but then I start wondering, did they miss something, did I fail to ask the right question, is there some hidden problem that’s going to cost more later? And then before I’ve even finished that thought, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are getting sloppy, whether I’m falling behind in what I’m supposed to cover, whether the students can tell I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then it branches out again. I worry about the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been giving them enough attention, or whether I’m letting them down. And then I’ll go home and I’m not even really present because I’m thinking about whether I’m disappointing my wife, my family, by not keeping everything under control the way I usually try to.\n\nSo yes… even when nothing is actively falling apart, I’m still scanning for what might be. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your anxiety or tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head. I tend to tell myself I’m prioritizing, or being realistic, or that I’ll get back to things once things calm down. But if I’m honest, I’ve stopped taking on some things because I feel too tense and overwhelmed to manage them.\n\nAt school, for example, I’m falling behind on lesson prep and grading in a way that really bothers me. I used to be the kind of teacher who had materials ready ahead of time and could keep the grading moving along steadily. Lately I’ll put it off because just looking at the stack or opening the planning file makes me feel like I’m already behind before I’ve even started. So I avoid it for a while, which of course makes it worse.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve pulled back from parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I didn’t quit everything, but I gave up some of the responsibilities I normally would have handled because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I felt bad doing that, because people rely on me, and I’m used to being someone who says yes and figures it out. But lately the thought of adding one more thing to coordinate just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying focused on a few specific areas, like work or home, or does it seem to spread across many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It spreads. I mean, it may start with one thing—usually the house lately, because the contractor situation is right there in front of me—but it doesn’t stay there.\n\nI’ll be thinking, “What if they missed something? What if I didn’t catch some detail I should have asked about?” And then somehow that turns into school, like, “Are my lesson plans getting thin? Am I slipping and just not noticing it?” Then I’ll start thinking about the robotics kids and whether they’re falling behind because I haven’t been as available or as organized with them.\n\nAnd then it follows me home, too. I’ll be sitting there with my wife and family, but in my head I’m still going over whether I’m letting everyone down because I can’t keep all the pieces under control the way I usually try to. So it’s not just one neat category. It kind of jumps tracks from the house to work to students to family, and by the time I notice it, I’ve been worrying about half my life at once.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel completely free from worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I keep trying to think of one area where I can say, “No, that’s fine, I’m not worried about that,” but my mind doesn’t seem to leave anything alone for very long.\n\nEven if I’m not actively dealing with one problem, it sort of moves on to the next. Like, I’ll start with the house and whether the contractors missed something or whether I failed to catch some detail. Then before I know it, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not keeping up the way I should. And then it goes to the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been as on top of that as I normally am.\n\nAnd then at home, instead of feeling like I can just relax, I start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So… no, I don’t really have a place where I feel completely free of it. It feels like it follows me around and just changes subjects.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious in social situations, like when you're with your colleagues or in a group of people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say social situations are the main thing for me. I mean, I can be tense around colleagues sometimes, especially if there’s a meeting or a group conversation where I feel like I should have an answer ready, but I don’t know that I avoid people because of that specifically.\n\nWith colleagues, I’m usually able to function. I talk, I answer questions, I do what I need to do. It’s more that I might be in my head while I’m doing it, wondering if I’m coming across as scattered or if I’ve forgotten something I was supposed to follow up on. But being with people itself isn’t usually the thing that sets it off, at least not in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, not especially in social situations just for the sake of being social. I haven’t really noticed it as a separate pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience almost every day, regardless of what's happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s pretty much almost every day. It doesn’t always feel like the same worry, if that makes sense, but the worrying itself is there most days.\n\nSome days it starts right away with the house—like I’ll be thinking about whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail before it became a problem. Then even if I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve thought about that enough,” my mind just shifts to school. I’ll start wondering if my lesson plans are getting thin, or if I’m slipping and not realizing it. Then it moves to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re falling behind because I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then even when I’m home, when nothing is actually happening in that moment, I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So yes, it’s not really dependent on one specific crisis happening. It’s more like my mind keeps finding something to latch onto, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you feel you can do without worrying about the outcome or potential problems?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and… honestly, not much comes to mind. Even things that should be simple or familiar, I seem to turn them into something I need to check or anticipate or prevent from going wrong.\n\nLike with the house, I can’t just think, “Okay, the work is being handled.” My mind goes straight to whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail earlier. And then if I try to put that aside, I don’t really feel free of the worry—it just switches tracks. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping or whether I’m not preparing as well as I should.\n\nThen there’s the robotics kids, and I’ll worry they’re behind because maybe I haven’t been as organized or as present as I normally am. And at home, even when I should be able to just sit with my wife and family, I start thinking I’m disappointing them because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can do things, I get through them, but doing them without worrying about the outcome or what could go wrong? That’s the part that feels hard to find.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing that's happening, or is it more of a general feeling that something could go wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think… more like a general feeling that something could go wrong, even if there isn’t one clear thing happening right then.\n\nI mean, there are specific topics my mind grabs onto, but it doesn’t stay neatly with one problem. It’ll start with the house, like wondering if the contractors missed something or if I should have caught some issue earlier. Then I’ll try to reason with myself and say, “Okay, there’s nothing you can do about that right now,” but then it just shifts to school. I’ll start thinking my lesson plans might be slipping, or that I’m not preparing the way I should.\n\nAnd then it moves again to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re behind because I haven’t been organized enough or attentive enough. Then at home I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo it’s not usually just one specific event. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for where something might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your anxiety is affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, especially with my family, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it’s not like there’s been some huge blowup or anything. It’s more that I’m not as present as I used to be.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that spills over at home because I’m bringing more of it back with me mentally. Even if I’m sitting there with my wife or trying to be with the family, part of me is still thinking, “I need to catch up, I’m behind, I’m letting things slide.” So I’m there, but I’m not really there, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd outside of work, I used to help manage parts of the community theater stuff—organizing things, keeping track of details, being one of the people others could rely on. I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated doing that, because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and those are people I care about.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting relationships, not necessarily because people are angry with me, but because I’m pulling back from things and not carrying what I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is interfering with your ability to enjoy time with friends or engage in hobbies you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, I still try to do things, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped functioning, but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nA big part of it is that I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that sort of hangs over everything. Even if I’m technically doing something enjoyable, part of my mind is still back at school thinking, “You should be catching up, you should be more prepared, you’re letting things pile up.” So it’s hard to actually enjoy the time, because I feel like I haven’t earned it, I guess.\n\nAnd with hobbies or community things… the community-theater work is probably the clearest example. I used to be the kind of teacher who had materials ready ahead of time and could keep the grading moving along steadily. Lately I’ll put it off because just looking at the stack or opening the planning file makes me feel like I’m already behind before I’ve even started. So I avoid it for a while, which of course makes it worse.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve pulled back from parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I didn’t quit everything, but I gave up some of the responsibilities I normally would have handled because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I felt bad doing that, because people rely on me, and I’m used to being someone who says yes and figures it out. But lately the thought of adding one more thing to coordinate just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying focused on a few specific areas, like work or home, or does it seem to spread across many different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It spreads. I mean, it may start with one thing—usually the house lately, because the contractor situation is right there in front of me—but it doesn’t stay there.\n\nI’ll be thinking, “What if they missed something? What if I didn’t catch some detail I should have asked about?” And then somehow that turns into school, like, “Are my lesson plans getting thin? Am I slipping and just not noticing it?” Then I’ll start thinking about the robotics kids and whether they’re falling behind because I haven’t been as available or as organized with them.\n\nAnd then it follows me home, too. I’ll be sitting there with my wife and family, but in my head I’m still going over whether I’m letting everyone down because I can’t keep all the pieces under control the way I usually try to. So it’s not just one neat category. It kind of jumps tracks from the house to work to students to family, and by the time I notice it, I’ve been worrying about half my life at once.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel completely free from worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I keep trying to think of one area where I can say, “No, that’s fine, I’m not worried about that,” but my mind doesn’t seem to leave anything alone for very long.\n\nEven if I’m not actively dealing with one problem, it sort of moves on to the next. Like, I’ll start with the house and whether the contractors missed something or whether I failed to catch some detail. Then before I know it, I’m thinking about school—whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not keeping up the way I should. And then it goes to the robotics kids, whether they’re behind because I haven’t been as on top of that as I normally am.\n\nAnd then at home, instead of feeling like I can just relax, I start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So… no, I don’t really have a place where I feel completely free of it. It feels like it follows me around and just changes subjects.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious in social situations, like when you're with your colleagues or in a group of people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say social situations are the main thing for me. I mean, I can be tense around colleagues sometimes, especially if there’s a meeting or a group conversation where I feel like I should have an answer ready, but I don’t know that I avoid people because of that specifically.\n\nWith colleagues, I’m usually able to function. I talk, I answer questions, I do what I need to do. It’s more that I might be in my head while I’m doing it, wondering if I’m coming across as scattered or if I’ve forgotten something I was supposed to follow up on. But being with people itself isn’t usually the thing that sets it off, at least not in a clear way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, not especially in social situations just for the sake of being social. I haven’t really noticed it as a separate pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience almost every day, regardless of what's happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s pretty much almost every day. It doesn’t always feel like the same worry, if that makes sense, but the worrying itself is there most days.\n\nSome days it starts right away with the house—like I’ll be thinking about whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail before it became a problem. Then even if I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve thought about that enough,” my mind just shifts to school. I’ll start wondering if my lesson plans are getting thin, or if I’m slipping and not realizing it. Then it moves to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re falling behind because I’m not as on top of things as I should be.\n\nAnd then even when I’m home, when nothing is actually happening in that moment, I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So yes, it’s not really dependent on one specific crisis happening. It’s more like my mind keeps finding something to latch onto, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you feel you can do without worrying about the outcome or potential problems?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and… honestly, not much comes to mind. Even things that should be simple or familiar, I seem to turn them into something I need to check or anticipate or prevent from going wrong.\n\nLike with the house, I can’t just think, “Okay, the work is being handled.” My mind goes straight to whether the contractors missed something, or whether I should have noticed some detail earlier. And then if I try to put that aside, I don’t really feel free of the worry—it just switches tracks. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping or whether I’m not preparing as well as I should.\n\nThen there’s the robotics kids, and I’ll worry they’re behind because maybe I haven’t been as organized or as present as I normally am. And at home, even when I should be able to just sit with my wife and family, I start thinking I’m disappointing them because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can do things, I get through them, but doing them without worrying about the outcome or what could go wrong? That’s the part that feels hard to find.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing that's happening, or is it more of a general feeling that something could go wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think… more like a general feeling that something could go wrong, even if there isn’t one clear thing happening right then.\n\nI mean, there are specific topics my mind grabs onto, but it doesn’t stay neatly with one problem. It’ll start with the house, like wondering if the contractors missed something or if I should have caught some issue earlier. Then I’ll try to reason with myself and say, “Okay, there’s nothing you can do about that right now,” but then it just shifts to school. I’ll start thinking my lesson plans might be slipping, or that I’m not preparing the way I should.\n\nAnd then it moves again to the robotics kids, like maybe they’re behind because I haven’t been organized enough or attentive enough. Then at home I’ll start worrying that I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nSo it’s not usually just one specific event. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for where something might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your anxiety is affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, especially with my family, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it’s not like there’s been some huge blowup or anything. It’s more that I’m not as present as I used to be.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that spills over at home because I’m bringing more of it back with me mentally. Even if I’m sitting there with my wife or trying to be with the family, part of me is still thinking, “I need to catch up, I’m behind, I’m letting things slide.” So I’m there, but I’m not really there, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd outside of work, I used to help manage parts of the community theater stuff—organizing things, keeping track of details, being one of the people others could rely on. I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated doing that, because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and those are people I care about.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting relationships, not necessarily because people are angry with me, but because I’m pulling back from things and not carrying what I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is interfering with your ability to enjoy time with friends or engage in hobbies you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I mean, I still try to do things, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped functioning, but it’s definitely gotten in the way.\n\nA big part of it is that I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that sort of hangs over everything. Even if I’m technically doing something enjoyable, part of my mind is still back at school thinking, “You should be catching up, you should be more prepared, you’re letting things pile up.” So it’s hard to actually enjoy the time, because I feel like I haven’t earned it, I guess.\n\nAnd with hobbies or community things… the community-theater work is probably the clearest example. I used to manage parts of that, the organizing and keeping track of details, and I liked being involved. It made me feel useful and connected. But I gave up parts of it because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I was worried I’d drop something or not follow through properly, and eventually it felt like I had to step back.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfering. Not because I don’t care about those things anymore — I do — but because I can’t seem to relax into them the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I’d say it has, more than I wanted to admit for a while.

It’s not that my whole life has fallen apart or anything like that, but the quality of it feels worse because I’m constantly trying to catch up. At school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, and that really bothers me because I’ve always tried to be dependable and prepared. When I’m behind, it follows me around. I don’t just leave it at school — I carry it into the evening, into weekends, into time I’m supposed to be with my family or doing something else.

And then with the community theater work, I gave up parts of what I usually manage because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. That was hard, honestly. I liked being someone who helped organize things and kept track of details. Stepping back made me feel like I wasn’t quite myself, or like I was shrinking my life down just to manage the anxiety.

So yes, overall, it’s affecting my quality of life. I’m still functioning, but it feels like I’m functioning with less room, less ease, and less enjoyment than I used to.
